- Nagabandha: figure-of-eight body connection.
- Music: pung drum central, flute, khartals, dhols; Jayadeva and Chandidas compositions.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 15th-century Vaishnavism; 18th-century revival by Bhag Chandra (owner). (✅ owner 09)
**Links:** ART-3014 (Nagabandha trap), ART-3016 (Odissi contrast), ART-3018 (Sattriya Vaishnava contrast).
**Trap:** Odissi and Manipuri both have devotional themes. Manipuri restrains facial abhinaya; Odissi foregrounds it.
**Mains hook:** Use the pung drum and veiled face to show that devotion can produce a restrained, not sensual, grammar.
**Back:** *"Manipuri: pung drum, veiled face, figure-of-eight Nagabandha."*

### Flashcard ART-3018 — Sattriya
**Front:** Where is Sattriya institutionally rooted, and what is its key identifier?
**Definition (learn):** Assam's classical dance linked to Sankaradeva's sattra (Vaishnava monastic institution) and ankiya-nat context, with mati-akhora as the movement base. (✅ owner 09 §5, §13.4)
**Breakdown:**
- Home: Assamese sattra (monastic) setting.
- Movement base: mati-akhora.
- Setting: monastic and devotional performance.
- Element and costume: owner gives none. ⚠️
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sankaradeva's sattra context (owner). CSE 2014 tests Sattriya (owner 09 §9).
**Links:** ART-3017 (both Vaishnava), ART-3008, ART-3007.
**Trap:** Confusing Sattriya with Manipuri because both are Vaishnava. The institutional home is the sattra.
**Mains hook:** Name the sattra when the question is about monastic performance.
**Back:** *"Sattriya: Assam's sattra, Sankaradeva, mati-akhora."*

### Flashcard ART-3019 — Garba of Gujarat and UNESCO ICH (folk, not SNA classical)
**Front:** What is Garba's recognition status, and what year did UNESCO inscribe it?
**Definition (learn):** Garba is a Gujarati folk dance. The owner records UNESCO inscription on the Representative List of the Intangible Cultural Heritage of Humanity in 2023. It is a folk form, not one of the SNA's eight classical forms. (✅ owner 09 §8) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Category: folk (living practice), not SNA classical.
- Recognition track: UNESCO ICH.
- Owner 09 asks that the year be verified against UNESCO's own element page.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Inscribed 2023 per owner. ⚠️ Wikipedia's Garba page (fetched) gives Gujarat origin but no inscription year, so the year is owner-only and unconfirmed.
**Links:** ART-3008, ART-3020, ART-3022, ART-3187 (Deepavali ICH, file 13).
**Trap:** Calling Garba a classical dance; or treating its UNESCO listing as SNA recognition.
**Mains hook:** Garba is the owner's example of a folk living tradition with an international listing; use it to separate the two recognition tracks.
**Back:** *"Garba: Gujarat folk, UNESCO living heritage, not SNA classical."*

### Flashcard ART-3020 — Trap: SNA classical recognition versus UNESCO ICH
**Front:** Are SNA classical-form status and UNESCO ICH inscription the same kind of recognition?
**Definition (learn):** No. SNA classical status is national academy recognition of a classical form. UNESCO ICH inscription is an international treaty-based listing of living practice. A form may hold one, both or neither. (✅ owner 09 §9, advanced §9)
**Breakdown:**
- SNA: national, classical canon.
- UNESCO ICH: international, living practice, folk or classical.
- Owner 09 flags that these are governed by different bodies and criteria.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Garba (UNESCO 2023, owner); Kutiyattam (UNESCO 2008, owner, ART-3033).
**Links:** ART-3008, ART-3019, ART-3022.
**Trap:** The examiner's shortcut "UNESCO-listed, therefore classical."
**Mains hook:** Close a recognition answer with a dated status line for each track, not one undated claim.
**Back:** *"SNA says classical, UNESCO says living: two ledgers, not one."*

### Flashcard ART-3021 — Trap: Lasya and Tandava are not a gender rule
**Front:** Do Lasya and Tandava assign dance styles to particular genders?
**Definition (learn):** No. They denote aspects or qualities present in dance generally, with varying emphasis. (✅ owner 09 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Lasya: graceful, expressive.
- Tandava: rhythmic, forceful.
- Neither is exclusive to one gender in performance.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None; conceptual trap.
**Links:** ART-3003, ART-3014 (Mohiniyattam is a solo lasya form).
**Trap:** "Lasya is feminine, Tandava masculine." Reject it.
**Mains hook:** Use qualities, not genders, when analysing a dancer figure.
**Back:** *"Qualities, not genders."*

### Flashcard ART-3022 — 2024 Prelims: latest Indian ICH inclusion (recognition-status control)
**Front:** What does the 2024 Prelims question on the UNESCO ICH list test?
**Definition (learn):** The routed 2024 Prelims GS-I Q60 demand asks for the latest Indian inclusion in the UNESCO Intangible Cultural Heritage List. The owner records the official Set-A key as available locally but does not record the answer in this integration. (✅ owner 09 PYQ block) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Type: date-sensitive objective item.
- Source status: key held locally, answer not recorded.
- Use: recognition-status discipline (ART-3020).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Latest inclusion as of 2024 per owner; ⚠️ the current latest inclusion must be verified against UNESCO.
**Links:** ART-3019, ART-3020, ART-3187 (Deepavali, 2025).
**Trap:** Answering from memory without a dated source; ICH totals and "latest" claims change.
**Mains hook:** Any "latest inscription" claim needs a dated UNESCO source.
**Back:** *"Latest changes; check the date before you answer."*

### Flashcard ART-3023 — Dance and sculpture share one gestural vocabulary
**Front:** Which sites show dancers, and why does dance vocabulary help read temple sculpture?
**Definition (learn):** Sculptures of dancers at Khajuraho, Konark, Ellora and Chidambaram depict poses associated with classical forms. The Natya Shastra unites dance and sculpture under one vocabulary. (✅ owner 09 §13.7, PDF p. 473; owner 09 advanced §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Sites: Khajuraho, Konark, Ellora, Chidambaram.
- Shared vocabulary: mudra, karana, tribhanga, Tandava and Lasya.
- Limit: sculpture proves a movement idiom existed; it does not prove a named form was performed (owner 09 §13.7).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 473. (✅ owner 09)
**Links:** ART-3001, ART-3006, ART-3016, ART-3024.
**Trap:** "Sculptures show dancers, so classical dance is ancient." The owner rejects this inference.
**Mains hook:** Read the figure with the same vocabulary as the dance, then state what the stone cannot prove.
**Back:** *"Same gestures in stone and on stage, but stone shows idiom, not choreography."*

### Flashcard ART-3024 — Tribhanga: Odissi, Amaravati and the Harappan dancing girl
**Front:** What is tribhanga and what links it to Amaravati and the Harappan dancing girl?
**Definition (learn):** Tribhanga is the three-bend posture innate to Odissi. Owner 09 says the same three-bend posture is used extensively by the Amaravati sculptors and appears in the Harappan Dancing Girl. (✅ owner 09 §13.7) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Odissi: tribhanga as core posture.
- Amaravati sculpture: same three-bend posture (owner).
- Harappan Dancing Girl: cross-referenced (owner, topic 06).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** No dates given in the owner for the Amaravati link. ⚠️
**Links:** ART-3016, ART-3023.
**Trap:** Claiming the posture proves a continuous unchanged dance tradition from Harappa to Odisha. The owner does not claim it.
**Mains hook:** Use tribhanga as a posture across periods, and state that continuity of posture is not continuity of performance.
**Back:** *"Tribhanga: three bends, Odisha to Amaravati, a posture not a lineage."*

### GROUP — Theatre, Puppetry and Performance Traditions

### Flashcard ART-3025 — Theatre and puppetry as performance archive (concept card)
**Front:** What is the core structure of Indian theatre and puppetry in this owner?
**Definition (learn):** Classical Sanskrit theatre is governed by codified conventions (Lokadharmi/Natyadharmi, ten rupakas, stage roles, language registers). Folk and modern theatre arose later and regionally. Puppetry is classified into four mechanisms: string, shadow, rod and glove. (✅ owner 10 §§2-5)
**Breakdown:**
- Sanskrit theatre: conventions and characters (ART-3026 to ART-3031).
- Decline and survival: ART-3032, ART-3033.
- Folk and modern: ART-3034 to ART-3043.
- Puppetry: ART-3044 to ART-3050.
- Martial and ritual performance: ART-3051.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four puppetry categories (owner 10 §3, PDF p. 541-ff).
**Links:** ART-3008 (dance), ART-3026, ART-3044.
**Trap:** Treating "Indian theatre" as one tradition. The owner separates classical, folk, modern and puppetry.
**Mains hook:** Start with the category, then the form and region.
**Back:** *"Classical rules, folk roots, modern stages, four puppet mechanisms."*

### Flashcard ART-3026 — Lokadharmi versus Natyadharmi
**Front:** Distinguish lokadharmi from natyadharmi.
**Definition (learn):** Lokadharmi is the realistic depiction of daily life. Natyadharmi is conventional, stylised and symbolic narration. (✅ owner 10 §2, PDF p. 501)
**Breakdown:**
- Lokadharmi: realism.
- Natyadharmi: stylisation and convention.
- Both are modes within the Natya Shastra's framework.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 501. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3002, ART-3027, ART-3028.
**Trap:** Equating lokadharmi with "folk theatre" or natyadharmi with "Sanskrit only." These are modes, not genres.
**Mains hook:** Use the pair to explain why stage realism was a choice, not a default.
**Back:** *"Lokadharmi shows life as it is; natyadharmi shows life as convention."*

### Flashcard ART-3027 — The ten rupakas
**Front:** List the ten rupakas (play types).
**Definition (learn):** Ten play types in Sanskrit dramaturgy: Nataka, Prakarana, Bhana, Prahasana, Dima, Vyayoga, Samavakara, Vithi, Ihamriga and Utsrishtikanka. (✅ owner 10 §2, §13.4, PDF p. 501)
**Breakdown:**
- Two described in detail: Nataka and Prakarana (owner 10 §Semantic control).
- Remaining eight are the other rupakas.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ten types, owner list. ❌ Do not reconstruct spellings from faulty OCR (owner 10).
**Links:** ART-3026, ART-3030.
**Trap:** Saying "Nataka and Prakarana are all ten rupakas." They are two of ten.
**Mains hook:** Name the genre before describing a play.
**Back:** *"Ten rupakas, Nataka and Prakarana among them; not two of them alone."*

### Flashcard ART-3028 — Sutradhar (the stage manager and string-holder)
**Front:** Who is the Sutradhar, and how does the term also apply to puppetry?
**Definition (learn):** In classical Sanskrit theatre the Sutradhar is the stage manager or director who performs the preliminaries and introduces the play. In puppetry the term is also read as "holder of strings" and narrator. (✅ owner 10 §2, PDF pp. 502, 540-542)
**Breakdown:**
- Theatre role: preliminaries, prayers, invocation, announcing time and place (PDF p. 502).
- Puppetry role: narrator and string-holder.
- Owner warns that the two roles are related but not identical.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Sutradhar dressed in white (owner, PDF p. 502). ⚠️ That detail is in owner 10 §13.4 only.
**Links:** ART-3025, ART-3044, ART-3045.
**Trap:** Equating the Sanskrit stage-director with a puppet string-holder. Owner 10 says related, not identical.
**Mains hook:** Use Sutradhar to show that performance opened as ritual, not narrative.
**Back:** *"Sutradhar: prayers first, play next; strings also held in puppetry."*

### Flashcard ART-3029 — Nayaka, Nayika and Vidusaka
**Front:** What are the three broad character types of Sanskrit drama?
**Definition (learn):** Nayaka is the hero; Nayika is the heroine; Vidusaka is the comic or satirical clown who traditionally spoke Prakrit while others spoke Sanskrit. (✅ owner 10 §2, PDF p. 503)
**Breakdown:**
- Nayaka: hero, with types Lalita, Shanta, Uddhata; may be a Pratinayaka antagonist.
- Nayika: queens, friends, courtesans (Ganika), divine lady (Divya).
- Vidusaka: noble, good-hearted comic friend who satirises social norms.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp. 502-503. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3078 (language register as social marker), ART-3030.
**Trap:** Calling Vidusaka the villain. He is a comic friend.
**Mains hook:** Use the Vidusaka's Prakrit as evidence that speech marked social position on stage.
**Back:** *"Hero, heroine, comic friend who speaks Prakrit."*

### Flashcard ART-3030 — Sanskrit drama's conventions: happy endings and no tragedy
**Front:** What did classical Sanskrit plays generally do about tragedy and the hero's fate?
**Definition (learn):** Per the book, plays were generally four to seven acts, always had happy endings, rarely portrayed tragedy, and the protagonist was male and attained his desire. (✅ owner 10 §3, PDF p. 502)
**Breakdown:**
- Length: four to seven acts.
- Ending: happy (the hero wins or does not die).
- Tragedy: almost rare.
- Protagonist: male, attains desire.
- Structure: opening, progression, development, pause, conclusion.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 502. ⚠️ Owner treats these as prescriptive conventions, not a census of every performance.
**Links:** ART-3031, ART-3027, ART-3025.
**Trap:** Saying Sanskrit plays ended in tragedy like Greek drama. The book says the opposite.
**Mains hook:** State the convention, then say it was prescriptive and not universal.
**Back:** *"Four to seven acts, happy ending, hero wins or lives."*

### Flashcard ART-3031 — Staging prescriptions: 400 persons, two storeys, no masks
**Front:** What staging details does Bharata's text prescribe?
**Definition (learn):** The text prescribes a theatre for about 400 persons, two-storeyed stages (upper for celestial, lower for terrestrial), curtains that intensify impact and no masks. (✅ owner 10 §13.4, PDF p. 502)
**Breakdown:**
- Capacity: about 400 (prescription).
- Stage: two storeys.
- Curtains: heighten impact.
- Masks: not used (for classical Sanskrit theatre only).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 502. ⚠️ Prescription, not an archaeological description (owner 10 §13.4).
**Links:** ART-3002, ART-3030, ART-3033 (Kutiyattam uses its own conventions).
**Trap:** Saying every stage held 400 or that masks were universal. Owner rejects both as universal claims.
**Mains hook:** Distinguish what the text prescribes from what excavation shows.
**Back:** *"400 seats, two floors, no masks, as prescribed."*

### Flashcard ART-3032 — Three named causes of Sanskrit theatre's decline
**Front:** What causes does the book give for the decline of Sanskrit theatre?
**Definition (learn):** Writers moved from plays to poems and songs; strict rules and difficult Sanskrit reduced popularity while Pali and Prakrit became common; under Muslim rulers dance and music were patronised more than theatre. (✅ owner 10 §13.4, PDF p. 503)
**Breakdown:**
- Authorial shift: plays to poems and songs.
- Linguistic: strict rules, difficult Sanskrit, rise of Pali and Prakrit.
- Patronage: dance and music favoured over theatre.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 503. ⚠️ Owner 10 advanced warns the list should be verified line by line before citing each as an exam fact.
**Links:** ART-3033, ART-3054 (completed versus ongoing decline), ART-3078 (language register).
**Trap:** Treating the three causes as alternatives. Owner says they are not mutually exclusive.
**Mains hook:** Argue three mechanisms and note that the Kutiyattam survival complicates "dead theatre."
**Back:** *"Poems not plays, hard Sanskrit, patrons went to dance."*

### Flashcard ART-3033 — Kutiyattam (living Sanskrit theatre, Kerala)
**Front:** What is Kutiyattam and why is it important?
**Definition (learn):** Kerala's Sanskrit theatre tradition, described as India's oldest continuing form of theatre, adhering to Natya Shastra rules, surviving since the 10th century AD, traditionally the privilege of the Chakyar and Nambiar castes. (✅ owner 10 §13.4, PDF pp. 503-504)
**Breakdown:**
- Languages: Sanskrit, Prakrit and Malayalam.
- Instruments: Mizhavu (drum), Edakka.
- Structure: every character begins with Nirvahana, a recollection of past events.
- Exponent: Margi Madhu Chakyar (leading exponent, per owner).
- Recognition: UNESCO ICH (owner: 2008, Representative List).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Surviving since the 10th century AD (owner). UNESCO: owner says 2008. ⚠️ CONFLICT: Wikipedia's Kutiyattam infobox (fetched) gives "2001 (3rd session)" and calls it a Masterpiece of Oral and Intangible Heritage. The 2001 date is the Masterpiece proclamation; the owner's 2008 is the Representative List date. Both must be dated separately; verify on UNESCO.
**Links:** ART-3032, ART-3020, ART-3031.
**Trap:** Using "2001" or "2008" without saying which list. The question's list decides the year.
**Mains hook:** Kutiyattam refutes "Sanskrit theatre is dead" and is the strongest living-practice safeguarding case in this owner.
**Back:** *"Kutiyattam: Kerala, Nirvahana, Mizhavu, living since the 10th century."*

### Flashcard ART-3034 — Folk theatre: origin, function and three-stage arc
**Front:** Describe the arc of Indian folk theatre.
**Definition (learn):** Folk theatre reflects local lifestyle, norms, beliefs and customs. It has rural roots and rustic style. Most forms began in the 15th-16th century with devotional themes, later added love stories and local heroes, and after Independence carried social lessons. (✅ owner 10 §13.5, PDF p. 504)
**Breakdown:**
- Stage 1: devotional.
- Stage 2: romantic and heroic.
- Stage 3: social message after Independence.
- Regional examples named in the owner's Prelims set: Bhand Pather, Swang, Maach, Bhaona, Mudiyettu, Dashavatar (⚠️ the owner does not describe each).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 15th-16th century (owner). ⚠️ The owner notes most forms began in this period; no per-form date.
**Links:** ART-3025, ART-3054, ART-3051.
**Trap:** Calling folk theatre a sophisticated urban form. The owner contrasts it with urban Sanskrit theatre.
**Mains hook:** Use the three-stage arc as the causal structure for "theatre as social change."
**Back:** *"Devotion, then romance and heroes, then social message."*

### Flashcard ART-3035 — Dramatic Performances Act, 1876
**Front:** What did the Dramatic Performances Act (1876) do?
**Definition (learn):** A colonial law imposed to curb political themes in theatre. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF p. 537)
**Breakdown:**
- Purpose: curb political content.
- Parallel: the 1927 Cinematograph Committee (owner, topic 15 cross-link).
- Evidence that theatre was politically consequential.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1876. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3036, ART-3038.
**Trap:** Presenting theatre as apolitical in the colonial period.
**Mains hook:** Use the Act as direct evidence of theatre as a political medium under colonial rule.
**Back:** *"1876: the state legislated against stage politics."*

### Flashcard ART-3036 — Colonial-era modern theatre: Calcutta, Madras, Parsi theatre
**Front:** What are the named early venues and the Parsi theatre dates?
**Definition (learn):** Modern Indian theatre grew in the colonial era from Sanskrit and Western texts. Urban centres (Calcutta, Madras) led to proscenium theatre. The Calcutta Theatre / New Playhouse was founded 1775, active until 1808. Parsi theatre was famous in western India 1850s-1920s, in Gujarati and Marathi. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF p. 537)
**Breakdown:**
- Hybrid form: Sanskrit plus Western influence.
- Venues: Calcutta Theatre (1775-1808); Gaiety Theatre Madras (Raghupathy Venkaiah, first permanent cinema theatre there); Gaiety Theatre Shimla (since 1887).
- Parsi theatre: colourful backdrops, music, romance, humour, melodrama; from the 1930s producers moved to film.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1775; 1808; 1850s-1920s; 1887 (owner). ⚠️ Gaiety Madras date not given in owner.
**Links:** ART-3035, ART-3037, ART-3182 (Parsi community, file 13).
**Trap:** Dating Parsi theatre to the 1930s. Its peak was 1850s-1920s; film came later.
**Mains hook:** Parsi theatre is the bridge from commercial stage to early cinema.
**Back:** *"1775 New Playhouse; 1850-1920 Parsi stage; then film."*

### Flashcard ART-3037 — Tagore and literary theatre
**Front:** What is literary theatre in this owner, and what did Tagore write?
**Definition (learn):** Plays by literary authors that carried nationalist and social critique. Tagore wrote his first play, Valmiki Pratibha, at 20; other plays include Raktakarabi (Red Oleanders), Chitrangada and Post-Office. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF pp. 537-538)
**Breakdown:**
- Tagore: Valmiki Pratibha (first play, at 20); Raktakarabi; Chitrangada; Post-Office.
- Other figures: Prasanna Kumar Thakur, Girishchandra Ghosh, Dinabandhu Mitra (Nildarpan).
- Themes: nationalism, spirituality, socio-political issues.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Tagore at 20 (owner). ⚠️ The owner does not give the year of Valmiki Pratibha.
**Links:** ART-3036, ART-3038, ART-3043 (Girish Chandra Ghosh).
**Trap:** Treating Tagore's plays as only literary. The owner classes them as nationalist and social critique.
**Mains hook:** Literary theatre shows the stage as a vehicle of social critique.
**Back:** *"Tagore: Valmiki first, Red Oleanders next, nationalism on stage."*

### Flashcard ART-3038 — Indian People's Theatre Association (1943)
**Front:** What was the IPTA and what was its significance?
**Definition (learn):** The Indian People's Theatre Association (1943) was the cultural wing of the Communist Party. It was disbanded in 1947 but formative, staging plays on social themes such as the Bengal famine. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF p. 538)
**Breakdown:**
- Founded: 1943.
- Affiliation: Communist Party cultural wing.
- Associated people: Balraj Sahni, Prithviraj Kapoor, Bijon Bhattacharya, Ritwik Ghatak, Utpal Dutt.
- Present: Chhattisgarh, Punjab, West Bengal (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1943; disbanded 1947 (owner). (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3037, ART-3040, ART-3035.
**Trap:** Saying the IPTA still operates as a national body. The owner says it exists in some states.
**Mains hook:** IPTA is the clearest case of organised political theatre.
**Back:** *"1943 IPTA: Bengal famine on stage, disbanded 1947."*

### Flashcard ART-3039 — Prithvi Theatre (1944 touring, 1978 permanent)
**Front:** What is Prithvi Theatre and when was it founded?
**Definition (learn):** Founded in 1944 by Prithviraj Kapoor as a moving theatre with an entourage of 150 artists, which staged more than 2,000 plays. A permanent theatre opened in Mumbai in 1978. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF p. 538)
**Breakdown:**
- Model: touring first, permanent later.
- Entourage: 150 artists.
- Plays staged: more than 2,000 (owner).
- Permanent venue: Mumbai, 1978.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1944; 1978 (owner). (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3038, ART-3040.
**Trap:** Confusing Prithvi's touring origin (1944) with its Mumbai venue (1978).
**Mains hook:** Mobility was a distribution strategy before permanent venues.
**Back:** *"Prithvi: moved first in 1944, settled in 1978."*

### Flashcard ART-3040 — Institutions and regional revival: SNA, NSD, Kalakshetra Manipur, Chorus Repertory
**Front:** Which institutions and regional revival efforts does the owner name for post-1947 theatre?
**Definition (learn):** Sangeet Natak Akademi (1952) promoted the performing arts; the National School of Drama produced leading personalities; Kalakshetra Manipur was formed in 1969 by Heisnam Kanhailal to keep traditional theatre alive; Ratan Thiyam's Chorus Repertory Theatre (1976). (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF p. 538)
**Breakdown:**
- 1952: Sangeet Natak Akademi.
- NSD: parented by SNA, independent since 1975 (owner 10 §13.5).
- 1969: Kalakshetra Manipur (Heisnam Kanhailal).
- 1976: Chorus Repertory Theatre (Ratan Thiyam).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1952; 1969; 1975; 1976 (owner). (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3038, ART-3039, ART-3041.
**Trap:** Saying NSD is an independent SNA organ. Owner: independent since 1975.
**Mains hook:** Institutions moved theatre from patronage to training and grants; regional institutions re-embed traditional forms.
**Back:** *"1952 SNA, 1975 NSD independent, 1969 Kalakshetra Manipur, 1976 Chorus."*

### Flashcard ART-3041 — Badal Sircar and the Third Theatre
**Front:** What was the Third Theatre movement?
**Definition (learn):** Developed by Badal Sircar (1925-2011), pioneer of modern and street theatre. It is a movement of small, experimental, socially conscious plays with minimalist staging and direct actor-audience interaction. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF p. 539)
**Breakdown:**
- Person: Badal Sircar (1925-2011).
- Form: small, experimental, socially conscious.
- Staging: minimal; direct actor-audience interaction.
- Owner's strongest "theatre as social tool" unit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1925-2011 (owner).
**Links:** ART-3040, ART-3037, ART-3038.
**Trap:** Confusing "Third Theatre" with a third stage of Sanskrit drama. It is a modern experimental movement.
**Mains hook:** The Third Theatre is the owner's best example of theatre as a social instrument.
**Back:** *"Sircar's Third Theatre: small, bare, direct to the audience."*

### Flashcard ART-3042 — Binodini Dasi
**Front:** Who was Binodini Dasi and why is she significant?
**Definition (learn):** Binodini Dasi (1863-1941), "Notee Binodini," was a prima donna of Bengali theatre and the first woman in India to become a notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s. Her autobiography Amar Katha was published in 1913. She contributed to founding the Star Theatre, Kolkata, in 1883. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF pp. 538-540)
**Breakdown:**
- Role: prima donna, Bengali theatre.
- Milestone: notable woman stage performer in the 1870s.
- Writing: Amar Katha, 1913.
- Institution: Star Theatre, Kolkata, 1883.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1863-1941; 1913; 1883 (owner).
**Links:** ART-3043, ART-3041.
**Trap:** Calling her the first Indian actress without the qualifier "notable stage performer from the Bengal Presidency in the 1870s" (owner's wording).
**Mains hook:** Binodini is the owner's strongest gender-and-performance evidence unit.
**Back:** *"Binodini: 1870s stage star, Amar Katha 1913, Star Theatre 1883."*

### Flashcard ART-3043 — Girish Chandra Ghosh and the Great National Theatre (1872)
**Front:** What did Girish Chandra Ghosh found and contribute?
**Definition (learn):** Girish Chandra Ghosh (1844-1912) was largely responsible for the golden age of Bengali theatre and co-founded the Great National Theatre (1872), the first Bengali professional theatre company. He wrote nearly 40 plays. (✅ owner 10 §13.5A, PDF pp. 538-540)
**Breakdown:**
- Lifespan: 1844-1912.
- Company: Great National Theatre, 1872.
- Output: about 40 plays.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1844-1912; 1872 (owner).
**Links:** ART-3042, ART-3037.
**Trap:** Calling the Great National Theatre the first Indian theatre. The owner says first Bengali professional company.
**Mains hook:** Professionalisation of Bengali theatre began with commercial companies.
**Back:** *"Girish Ghosh: Great National Theatre 1872, forty plays."*

### Flashcard ART-3044 — Puppetry: four mechanisms (classification)
**Front:** Name India's four puppetry categories and their control mechanism.
**Definition (learn):** String puppets (marionettes) are suspended and manipulated by strings. Shadow puppets are leather or opaque cut-outs projected on a lit screen. Rod puppets are moved by rods from below or behind. Glove puppets are controlled by fingers inside a sleeve-like body. (✅ owner 10 §3, §13.6, PDF p. 541-ff)
**Breakdown:**
- String: strings, jointed.
- Shadow: light, screen, translucency.
- Rod: larger figures, rods.
- Glove: most intimate scale; single-performer viable.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four categories (owner).
**Links:** ART-3045, ART-3046, ART-3047, ART-3048.
**Trap:** Treating the four categories as stylistically interchangeable. Owner says each has its own construction and control.
**Mains hook:** Classify by mechanism first, then name a regional tradition.
**Back:** *"String strings, shadow screens, rod rods, glove fingers."*

### Flashcard ART-3045 — String puppetry: Kathputli, Kundhei and Gombeyatta
**Front:** Name three string-puppet traditions and their distinguishing features.
**Definition (learn):** Kathputli (Rajasthan) uses legless wooden dolls controlled by finger-tied strings. Kundhei or Kandhei Nacha (Odisha) has more joints and Odissi-influenced movement. Gombeyatta (Karnataka) is styled on Yakshagana characters and uses multiple puppeteers. (✅ owner 10 §4, §5, PDF pp. 542-545)
**Breakdown:**
- Kathputli: legless, finger-string control.
- Kundhei: more jointed, Odissi-influenced; triangular prop (owner 10 §9).
- Gombeyatta: Yakshagana style, multiple puppeteers.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp. 542-545. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3044, ART-3028, ART-3046 (Togalu Gombeyatta shadow form).
**Trap:** Mixing Gombeyatta (string) with Togalu Gombeyatta (shadow). Same state, different mechanism.
**Mains hook:** Name the region and technique together.
**Back:** *"Kathputli legless, Kundhei jointed, Gombeyatta Yakshagana-style."*

### Flashcard ART-3046 — Shadow puppetry: Tholu Bommalata, Togalu Gombeyatta and Ravana Chhaya
**Front:** Name three shadow-puppet traditions and their regions.
**Definition (learn):** Shadow puppets are leather or opaque cut-outs projected against a lit screen. Tholu Bommalata (Andhra Pradesh), Togalu Gombeyatta (Karnataka) and Ravana Chhaya (Odisha). (✅ owner 10 §4, §13.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Medium: light, screen, translucency.
- Colour treatment differs by tradition (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3044, ART-3045.
**Trap:** Describing shadow puppets as rod or glove. Their control is by light and screen.
**Mains hook:** Shadow puppetry is the light-based mechanism; name the medium.
**Back:** *"Tholu in Andhra, Togalu in Karnataka, Ravana in Odisha: light makes them."*

### Flashcard ART-3047 — Rod puppetry: Putul Nach and Yampuri
**Front:** Name the two rod-puppet traditions and their regions.
**Definition (learn):** Figures moved by rods from below or behind. Putul Nach (West Bengal) and Yampuri (Bihar). (✅ owner 10 §4, §13.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Larger figures, standing manipulation.
- Control: rods.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3044, ART-3048.
**Trap:** Confusing rod with string. Strings hang from above; rods come from below.
**Mains hook:** Rod puppets are the larger-scale mechanism.
**Back:** *"Putul in Bengal, Yampuri in Bihar: rods from below."*

### Flashcard ART-3048 — Glove puppetry: Pavakoothu (Kerala)
**Front:** Which tradition is glove puppetry in this owner, and what is distinctive about it?
**Definition (learn):** Pavakoothu (Kerala). Head and arms controlled by fingers inside a sleeve-like body. (✅ owner 10 §4, §13.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Mechanism: fingers inside sleeve-like body.
- Scale: most intimate; single-performer viable (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3044, ART-3047.
**Trap:** Glove puppetry is not the same as string puppetry, even in Kerala-based questions.
**Mains hook:** Glove puppetry is the intimate scale in the four-mechanism grid.
**Back:** *"Pavakoothu: fingers inside the sleeve."*

### Flashcard ART-3049 — Puppetry's antiquity and archive value
**Front:** What evidence gives puppetry antiquity and a philosophical dimension?
**Definition (learn):** Puppetry's Indian antiquity is evidenced from Harappa and Mohenjo-daro, with references in the Mahabharata and the Tamil Silappadikaram. Marionette theatre is from 500 BC. The Bhagavad Gita's metaphor of God as puppeteer gives it a philosophical dimension. (✅ owner 10 §4, §13.6, PDF pp. 540-548)
**Breakdown:**
- Harappa/Mohenjo-daro evidence.
- Textual references: Mahabharata, Silappadikaram.
- Marionette theatre from 500 BC.
- Philosophy: Bhagavad Gita puppeteer metaphor.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 500 BC; PDF pp. 540-548. ⚠️ The owner's Harappan puppetry claim is an interpretation; verify before citing as settled.
**Links:** ART-3050, ART-3028.
**Trap:** Treating puppetry as only children's entertainment. Owner rejects that.
**Mains hook:** Use the archive framing to argue puppetry as a transmission vehicle for epic material.
**Back:** *"Puppets carried the epics and the Gita's puppeteer metaphor."*

### Flashcard ART-3050 — Puppetry's decline and the 2020 SNA Puraskar (Anupama Hoskere, Dhaatu)
**Front:** Why is puppetry in decline, and what dated safeguarding example does the owner give?
**Definition (learn):** The book documents puppetry as currently in decline "because of lack of funding and captive audience." The owner gives the SNA Puraskar 2020 to Anupama Hoskere, through her organisation Dhaatu, as a dated revival example. (✅ owner 10 §4, §13.6, PDF pp. 540-548)
**Breakdown:**
- Cause: funding and audience shortage.
- Response: named artist, organisation and dated award.
- Owner's chain: problem to named actor to dated recognition.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** SNA Puraskar 2020 (owner). ⚠️ Owner 10 advanced says verify any more recent SNA puppetry award against SNA's list.
**Links:** ART-3054, ART-3020, ART-3044.
**Trap:** Generalising the Dhaatu example to all four puppetry categories. Owner says not without qualification.
**Mains hook:** Use the cause-response-example chain and name the funding mechanism.
**Back:** *"Puppetry dies of empty seats and funds; Hoskere's 2020 Puraskar answers."*

### Flashcard ART-3051 — Martial and ritual performance traditions
**Front:** Which martial traditions does the owner map, and by what criteria?
**Definition (learn):** Kalaripayattu (Kerala), Silambam (Tamil Nadu), Thang-ta (Manipur), Gatka (Punjab), Mardani Khel (Maharashtra) and Pari-khanda or Chhau-linked traditions (eastern India). Map by weapon, movement and community, not one "ancient martial art." (✅ owner 10 §4, §13.7, PDF pp. 552-560)
**Breakdown:**
- Kalaripayattu: weapon and body training, living lineage.
- Silambam: staff-based.
- Thang-ta: sword and spear, choreographed display.
- Gatka: Sikh martial practice and demonstration.
- Mardani Khel: regional armed practice.
- Pari-khanda or Chhau: martial movement in masked dance-drama.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp. 552-560. ⚠️ Antiquity claims in oral lineages need separate checking.
**Links:** ART-3025, ART-3053, ART-3054.
**Trap:** Calling them interchangeable war dances.
**Mains hook:** Continuum between combat and display; describe by weapon, movement, community.
**Back:** *"Six traditions, six weapons, six communities: not one art."*

### Flashcard ART-3052 — Sitabenga and Jogimara: the disputed "oldest amphitheatre"
**Front:** What is the status of the claim that Sitabenga and Jogimara are the world's oldest amphitheatre?
**Definition (learn):** Archaeological spaces and inscriptions in Chhattisgarh linked by some scholars to performance. The popular "world's oldest amphitheatre" label is disputed and must not be stated as settled. (✅ owner 10 §2, §13.9)
**Breakdown:**
- Sites: Sitabenga and Jogimara caves.
- Claim: popular label, disputed.
- Use: mention as contested.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None settled. ⚠️
**Links:** ART-3025, ART-3031.
**Trap:** Stating the "oldest amphitheatre" label as fact.
**Mains hook:** Treat a popular archaeological label as a claim to test.
**Back:** *"Sitabenga: a claim, not a certificate."*

### Flashcard ART-3053 — PYQ routes: CSE 2011 regional theatre and CSE 2014 Kalaripayattu
**Front:** Which local PYQs route to this owner's content?
**Definition (learn):** CSE 2011 asks regional theatre forms such as Bhand Pather, Swang, Maach, Bhaona, Mudiyettu and Dashavatar. CSE 2014 identifies Kalaripayattu as a living martial tradition of South India. (✅ owner 10 §9)
**Breakdown:**
- Regional theatre: six named forms (2011).
- Martial: Kalaripayattu (2014).
- Owner: zero direct question-level route for the core owner itself; these are adjacent routes.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** CSE 2011; CSE 2014. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3034, ART-3051.
**Trap:** Matching Kalaripayattu to a North Indian martial art.
**Mains hook:** Use the named regional form, not a generic folk label.
**Back:** *"2011: six regional theatres. 2014: Kalaripayattu, South India."*

### Flashcard ART-3054 — Two kinds of decline: completed versus ongoing
**Front:** How should one distinguish Sanskrit theatre's decline from puppetry's decline?
**Definition (learn):** Classical Sanskrit theatre's decline is a completed historical process with identifiable causes. Puppetry's decline is current and documented, a crisis of funding and audience with active safeguarding. They need different responses. (✅ owner 10 advanced §1, §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Completed: Sanskrit theatre (causes: ART-3032).
- Ongoing: puppetry (cause and response: ART-3050).
- Living survival: Kutiyattam (ART-3033) shows contraction and continuity can coexist.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only analytical distinction. (✅ owner 10)
**Links:** ART-3032, ART-3033, ART-3050.
**Trap:** Saying "Sanskrit theatre is dead." Kutiyattam refutes it.
**Mains hook:** Classify the decline type before proposing a safeguarding measure.
**Back:** *"Sanskrit theatre declined and ended; puppetry is declining and can be saved."*

### GROUP — Languages, Scripts, Literature and Manuscripts

### Flashcard ART-3055 — Languages, scripts and manuscripts as one evidence system (concept card)
**Front:** How should language, script, literary register and manuscript evidence be organised in an answer?
**Definition (learn):** Language family, language, script, literary register, constitutional status and documentary-heritage recognition are separate classifications. Each has its own date and its own evidence. (✅ owner 11 §2, §9, advanced §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Classification: language families (ART-3056).
- Constitutional statuses (ART-3058 to ART-3060, ART-3089).
- Registers: Sanskrit, Prakrit, Pali (ART-3061 to ART-3065).
- Scripts: ART-3066 to ART-3070.
- Literature corpora: ART-3071 to ART-3081.
- Documentary heritage: ART-3082 to ART-3087.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only synthesis. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3057, ART-3089, ART-3078.
**Trap:** Treating family, script, register and status as one classification.
**Mains hook:** Name which classification a claim belongs to before supporting it.
**Back:** *"Family, script, register, status: four different questions."*

### Flashcard ART-3056 — The four major language families of India
**Front:** Name the major language families of India.
**Definition (learn):** Indo-Aryan (Indo-European), Dravidian, Austroasiatic and Tibeto-Burman. A language family is a group of languages related through a shared ancestor predating recorded history. (✅ owner 11 §2, §3, PDF p. 581)
**Breakdown:**
- Indo-Aryan: Old (Vedic and Classical Sanskrit), Middle (Pali, Prakrits), New (regional languages).
- Dravidian, Austroasiatic, Tibeto-Burman: listed as major families.
- Dialect: a local form of a language (Braj, Awadhi, Haryanvi as Hindi dialects, owner PDF p. 581).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Four families. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3057, ART-3062.
**Trap:** Calling Sanskrit a Dravidian or a separate family. It is Indo-Aryan.
**Mains hook:** No family is culturally more Indian; name the family, then the register.
**Back:** *"Indo-Aryan, Dravidian, Austroasiatic, Tibeto-Burman."*

### Flashcard ART-3057 — Firewall: family, dialect, script, register, status
**Front:** Which statements about language classification are wrong?
**Definition (learn):** Language families classify languages. Scripts (Brahmi, Kharoshthi, Devanagari, Grantha, Vatteluttu) are writing systems. Registers (Sanskrit, Prakrit) are social and literary varieties. Statuses (Eighth Schedule, official language, classical) are legal or honorific categories. (✅ owner 11 §13.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Family: Indo-Aryan etc.
- Script: Brahmi etc.
- Register: codified Sanskrit, common Prakrit.
- Status: legal (Eighth Schedule), official (Article 343), classical (honorific).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the list. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3055, ART-3056, ART-3089.
**Trap:** Assuming one family uses one script, or that a classical language is an Eighth Schedule language. Both are wrong.
**Mains hook:** Build each answer on the correct classification; mixing them is the commonest error.
**Back:** *"Family classifies tongues, script writes them, register speaks them, status names them."*

### Flashcard ART-3058 — Eighth Schedule: 22 Scheduled Languages and their additions
**Front:** How many Scheduled Languages does the Eighth Schedule list, and when were the later groups added?
**Definition (learn):** The Eighth Schedule lists 22 Scheduled Languages, including Sanskrit. The original 14 were in 1950; Sindhi was added in 1967 (21st Amendment); Konkani, Manipuri and Nepali in 1992 (71st Amendment); Bodo, Dogri, Santali and Maithili in 2003 (92nd Amendment). (✅ Wikipedia: Eighth Schedule to the Constitution of India; ✅ owner 11 §2)
**Breakdown:**
- 1950: Assamese, Bengali, Gujarati, Hindi, Kannada, Kashmiri, Malayalam, Marathi, Odia, Punjabi, Sanskrit, Tamil, Telugu, Urdu (14).
- 1967: Sindhi.
- 1992: Konkani, Manipuri, Nepali.
- 2003: Bodo, Dogri, Santali, Maithili.
- 2011: 96th Amendment changed Oriya to Odia; no new language.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 22 total; years above. (✅ Wikipedia)
**Links:** ART-3059, ART-3060, ART-3089, ART-3081 (Hindi).
**Trap:** Calling the Eighth Schedule "the official-language list." It lists Scheduled Languages; official language is a different status (ART-3059).
**Mains hook:** Scheduled status concerns recognition and development, not classical antiquity; say which.
**Back:** *"Fourteen in 1950, then Sindhi 1967, three in 1992, four in 2003: twenty-two."*

### Flashcard ART-3059 — Official language (Article 343) and the "national language" myth
**Front:** Is India's official language a constitutionally declared national language?
**Definition (learn):** No. Hindi in Devanagari is the official language of the Union under Article 343. The Constitution declares no national language. Article 343 status is distinct from Eighth Schedule and classical status. (✅ owner 11 §9, §13.9)
**Breakdown:**
- Article 343: official language of the Union, Hindi (Devanagari).
- No "national language" declaration exists.
- Eighth Schedule (22) is separate (ART-3058).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Article 343. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3058, ART-3089.
**Trap:** Saying "Hindi is the national language." The owner marks this as the most common error.
**Mains hook:** Separate official-language use from national identity; detailed doctrine belongs to Polity (owner 11 §11).
**Back:** *"Hindi is the official language, not a declared national language."*

### Flashcard ART-3060 — Classical languages: eleven after the October 2024 additions
**Front:** How many classical languages does India have, and which were added in October 2024?
**Definition (learn):** Owner 11 states that India has 11 classical languages after Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese and Bengali were added in October 2024 to Tamil, Sanskrit, Kannada, Telugu, Malayalam and Odia. The owner cites PIB, verified 21 July 2026. Classical status is not Eighth Schedule or official-language status. (✅ owner 11 §2, §13.7) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Original six: Tamil, Sanskrit, Kannada, Telugu, Malayalam, Odia.
- Added October 2024: Marathi, Pali, Prakrit, Assamese, Bengali.
- Total: 11.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 11; October 2024 (owner citing PIB). ⚠️ A live fetch of a Wikipedia page on classical-language status returned not found, so the count is owner-only and must be re-checked against PIB or the official list before citing.
**Links:** ART-3058, ART-3089, ART-3062 (Pali), ART-3085.
**Trap:** Saying "six classical languages" (the pre-2024 total).
**Mains hook:** Give the date and the list, and note that classical status is honorific, not constitutional.
**Back:** *"Six, then five more in October 2024: eleven, not Eighth Schedule."*

### Flashcard ART-3061 — Sanskrit: Panini and the earliest datable inscriptions
**Front:** What are the datable milestones of Sanskrit grammar and inscriptions?
**Definition (learn):** Panini's Ashtadhyayi systematised Sanskrit grammar, conventionally dated around the mid-first millennium BCE. The earliest extensive datable Sanskrit inscription is Rudradaman I's Junagarh (Girnar) rock inscription, c. 150 AD. The Chebrolu inscription (Andhra Pradesh, 207 AD, discovered 2019) is the earliest datable South Indian Sanskrit inscription. (✅ owner 11 §4, PDF pp. 582-583)
**Breakdown:**
- Grammar: Panini, Ashtadhyayi.
- Epigraphy: Junagarh c. 150 AD.
- South: Chebrolu 207 AD.
- Gupta height: Mahakavyas and Khandakavyas (owner 11 §4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** c. 150 AD; 207 AD; discovered 2019 (owner). ⚠️ Panini's date is "conventional"; treat as a range.
**Links:** ART-3062, ART-3075, ART-3083.
**Trap:** Equating Sanskrit's literary antiquity with its inscriptional record.
**Mains hook:** Separate grammar, inscription and literature as three dates.
**Back:** *"Panini grammar; Junagarh c. 150 AD; Chebrolu 207 AD in the south."*

### Flashcard ART-3062 — Pali: canonical language of Theravada Buddhism
**Front:** What is Pali, and what is its relation to Prakrit?
**Definition (learn):** Pali is the canonical language of Theravada Buddhism. Its relationship to named Prakrits is not a simple branch chart. (✅ owner 11 §3, §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Role: Theravada canon.
- Category: Middle Indo-Aryan register.
- Relation: historical, not one-parent-one-child.
- Owner 11 lists Pali among the October 2024 classical additions (ART-3060).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the owner. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3063, ART-3064, ART-3060, ART-3072.
**Trap:** Saying Pali is an uncontested sub-branch of one Prakrit. The owner rejects this.
**Mains hook:** Pali shows canonical language is transmission history, not speech.
**Back:** *"Pali: canon of Theravada, not a single Prakrit branch."*

### Flashcard ART-3063 — Ardhamagadhi and the Svetambara Jain canon
**Front:** What is Ardhamagadhi, and with which tradition is it linked?
**Definition (learn):** Ardhamagadhi is a Middle Indo-Aryan register central to the Svetambara Jain canon, linked by tradition to Mahavira. (✅ owner 11 §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Register: Prakrit family.
- Tradition: Svetambara Jain canon.
- Link: Mahavira (by tradition, not secure).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3073, ART-3062.
**Trap:** Assigning Mahavira a single certain spoken language. The owner says no.
**Mains hook:** Canonical language is a claim about transmission.
**Back:** *"Ardhamagadhi: Svetambara Jain canon, by tradition Mahavira."*

### Flashcard ART-3064 — Shauraseni and Maharashtri
**Front:** What were Shauraseni and Maharashtri?
**Definition (learn):** Shauraseni was a major dramatic and literary Prakrit, also used in Jain texts. Maharashtri was a major literary Prakrit, historically important in the Deccan and western India. (✅ owner 11 §3, §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Shauraseni: dramatic and literary; Jain textual use.
- Maharashtri: literary; Deccan and western India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3065, ART-3062, ART-3063.
**Trap:** Confusing Maharashtri with Marathi. Maharashtri is a Prakrit; Marathi is a later New Indo-Aryan language.
**Mains hook:** Literary Prakrits were regional and social, not a single standard.
**Back:** *"Shauraseni on stage, Maharashtri in the Deccan: Prakrits, not Marathi."*

### Flashcard ART-3065 — Sanskrit and Prakrit as coexisting registers
**Front:** Are Sanskrit and Prakrit simply old and new versions of one language?
**Definition (learn):** No. Sanskrit is codified and elite; Prakrit is the natural common tongue. They coexisted with different social functions rather than one succeeding the other. (✅ owner 11 §9, §13.1)
**Breakdown:**
- Sanskrit: codified, elite, learned.
- Prakrit: natural, common, literary and dramatic.
- Relation: coexistence (owner, PDF p. 219-220 on Kushana usage, ART-3070).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3078, ART-3064, ART-3070.
**Trap:** "Prakrit came after Sanskrit." The owner rejects this sequence.
**Mains hook:** Sanskrit and Prakrit divided labour; the question is who used which register.
**Back:** *"Sanskrit codified and elite; Prakrit common and natural, side by side."*

### Flashcard ART-3066 — Trap: Ashokan edicts were not Sanskrit or Devanagari
**Front:** In which languages and scripts were the Ashokan edicts written?
**Definition (learn):** Mostly Prakrit in Brahmi. Kharoshthi in the north-west. Greek and Aramaic at Kandahar. (✅ owner 11 §5, §13.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Majority: Prakrit, Brahmi.
- North-west: Kharoshthi.
- Kandahar: Greek and Aramaic.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond the list. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3067, ART-3068.
**Trap:** Saying Ashokan edicts were in Sanskrit in Devanagari.
**Mains hook:** Script and language vary by region within one ruler's edicts.
**Back:** *"Ashoka: Prakrit in Brahmi, Kharoshthi north-west, Greek-Aramaic at Kandahar."*

### Flashcard ART-3067 — Brahmi: an ancestral script, not one unchanged modern script
**Front:** What is Brahmi, and what is the trap about it?
**Definition (learn):** Brahmi is a major ancestral script with many regional descendants. (✅ owner 11 §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Role: ancestral script of many Indian scripts.
- Trap: treating it as a single modern script.
- Used for most Ashokan edicts (ART-3066).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3066, ART-3057.
**Trap:** "Brahmi is one unchanged modern script."
**Mains hook:** Scripts descend and diversify; they do not stay fixed.
**Back:** *"Brahmi: the ancestor, not the modern script."*

### Flashcard ART-3068 — Kharoshthi: right-to-left in the north-west
**Front:** What is the direction of Kharoshthi, and where was it used?
**Definition (learn):** Kharoshthi runs right-to-left and was used especially in the north-west. (✅ owner 11 §5, §13.6)
**Breakdown:**
- Direction: right-to-left.
- Area: north-west.
- Example: Ashokan record in north-west (ART-3066).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3066, ART-3070.
**Trap:** Assuming all Indian scripts run left-to-right.
**Mains hook:** Script direction is a regional and historical fact.
**Back:** *"Kharoshthi: north-west, right to left."*

### Flashcard ART-3069 — Pallava-Grantha replaces Vatteluttu; bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit inscriptions
**Front:** What script change did the Pallava court make, and what bilingual culture did it produce?
**Definition (learn):** Vatteluttu was replaced by the Pallava-Grantha script in the Pallava court. Bilingual Tamil-Sanskrit Pallava inscriptions appear from the 7th century onward. (✅ owner 11 §13.4, PDF pp. 223, 588, 608)
**Breakdown:**
- Script change: Vatteluttu to Pallava-Grantha.
- Bilingual: Tamil and Sanskrit, from the 7th century.
- Limit: court usage, not all writing in the region (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7th century; PDF p. 608. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3077, ART-3057.
**Trap:** Generalising the script change to all regional writing.
**Mains hook:** The script change is a datable cultural intervention beyond "patronage of poets."
**Back:** *"Pallava court: Vatteluttu gave way to Grantha; Tamil and Sanskrit side by side."*

### Flashcard ART-3070 — Kushana multilingual coexistence
**Front:** What does the Kushana evidence show about scripts and languages?
**Definition (learn):** Kushana rulers used Greek, Kharoshthi and Brahmi scripts with Greek, Prakrit and Sanskritised registers. The pattern is coexistence, not linear replacement. (✅ owner 11 §3, §13.6, R.S. Sharma PDF pp. 219-220)
**Breakdown:**
- Scripts: Greek, Kharoshthi, Brahmi.
- Registers: Greek, Prakrit, Sanskritised.
- Lesson: multilingual states, not one language per state.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF pp. 219-220. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3065, ART-3068.
**Trap:** Assuming one language per state.
**Mains hook:** Multilingual administration is a standard early pattern.
**Back:** *"Kushana: four scripts' worth of coexistence, no single line."*

### Flashcard ART-3071 — Vedic corpus: layered oral-textual texts
**Front:** What is the date status of the Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas and early Upanishads?
**Definition (learn):** Vedic Sanskrit corpora are layered oral-textual collections, not single-date books. (✅ owner 11 §4)
**Breakdown:**
- Corpora: Vedas, Brahmanas, Aranyakas, early Upanishads.
- Language: Vedic Sanskrit.
- Date: layered, not single.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3083, ART-3082.
**Trap:** Giving the Rigveda a single composition date.
**Mains hook:** Layered composition makes the single-date question unanswerable.
**Back:** *"Vedic texts are layers, not books."*

### Flashcard ART-3072 — Buddhist canon, Jatakas and avadana literature
**Front:** Name the Buddhist literary corpora and their languages.
**Definition (learn):** Buddhist canon and avadana use Pali, Prakrits, Buddhist Hybrid Sanskrit and Sanskrit. Key titles: Tripitaka, Jatakas, Mahavastu, Divyavadana. (✅ owner 11 §4, §13.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Canon: Tripitaka (Pali).
- Narrative: Jatakas.
- Avadana: Mahavastu, Divyavadana.
- Languages: Pali, Prakrit, Hybrid Sanskrit, Sanskrit.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3062, ART-3073.
**Trap:** Calling all Buddhist texts Pali. Several are Sanskrit or hybrid.
**Mains hook:** Buddhist literature is multilingual; name the language with the title.
**Back:** *"Tripitaka, Jatakas, Mahavastu, Divyavadana: four titles, four languages."*

### Flashcard ART-3073 — Jain canon and narrative literature
**Front:** Name the Jain literary corpora and their languages.
**Definition (learn):** Jain canon and narrative use Ardhamagadhi and other Prakrits, and later Sanskrit, Apabhramsha and regional languages. Key titles: Agamas, Kalpasutra, Parishishtaparvan. (✅ owner 11 §4, §13.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Canon: Agamas (Ardhamagadhi).
- Narrative: Kalpasutra, Parishishtaparvan.
- Later: Sanskrit, Apabhramsha.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3063, ART-3072.
**Trap:** Assuming Jain canon is Sanskrit throughout.
**Mains hook:** Jain canon is a Prakrit corpus with later Sanskrit extensions.
**Back:** *"Agamas in Ardhamagadhi; Kalpasutra and Parishishtaparvan in the narrative."*

### Flashcard ART-3074 — Sangam corpus: powerful but bounded evidence
**Front:** What is the Sangam corpus, and what kind of historical source is it?
**Definition (learn):** Early Tamil literature compiled c. CE 300-600 with older layers. It illuminates towns, trade, crafts, war, social ranking and hero-stone culture, but is not a connected dynastic chronicle. (✅ owner 11 §4, §13.5, R.S. Sharma PDF pp. 256-258)
**Breakdown:**
- Compilation: c. CE 300-600.
- Themes: towns, trade, crafts, war, social ranking, hero-stones.
- Limit: no coherent dynastic chronicle.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** CE 300-600. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3075, ART-3076, ART-3071.
**Trap:** Treating the Sangam corpus as a dynastic history.
**Mains hook:** Rich social evidence, bounded chronology.
**Back:** *"Sangam: rich, layered, not a dynastic chronicle."*

### Flashcard ART-3075 — Classical Sanskrit authors and the 2024 Bhasa attribution
**Front:** Which classical Sanskrit authors does the owner list, and what does the 2024 question test?
**Definition (learn):** Bhasa, Kalidasa, Shudraka, Bharavi and Dandin are the named classical authors; each work-author pair must be verified. The 2024 Prelims GS-I Q63 asks about a work attributed to the playwright Bhasa. (✅ owner 11 §4, §5, PYQ block) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Authors: Bhasa, Kalidasa, Shudraka, Bharavi, Dandin.
- 2024 Q63: Bhasa work attribution; key held locally, answer not recorded in owner.
- Test: verify work-author pair, not the famous name.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024 Prelims Q63. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3077, ART-3076, ART-3061.
**Trap:** Pattern-matching a famous name to a work. Verify the pair.
**Mains hook:** Attribution is a precision test; cite the work, the author and the source.
**Back:** *"Bhasa's play is a pair to verify, not a famous name to guess."*

### Flashcard ART-3076 — 2021 Prelims: Bhavabhuti, Hastimalla and Kshemeshvara
**Front:** What does the 2021 Prelims Q41 test?
**Definition (learn):** The routed 2021 Prelims GS-I Q41 asks identification of Bhavabhuti, Hastimalla and Kshemeshvara. The official key was unavailable locally. (✅ owner 11 PYQ block) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Demand: identify the three names and their works.
- Source: key unavailable locally; no answer recorded.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2021 Q41. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3075, ART-3077.
**Trap:** Answering by pattern-matching famous names.
**Mains hook:** Attribution questions reward verification.
**Back:** *"2021 Q41: check each name's work before you answer."*

### Flashcard ART-3077 — Dandin, Avantisundari Katha and the 2024 Pallava literature demand
**Front:** How does Dandin's Avantisundari Katha support the 2024 Pallava question?
**Definition (learn):** The 2024 Q2 Mains demand asks the contribution of the Pallavas of Kanchi to art and literature (150 words). Dandin, associated with the Pallava court, praised artists who repaired a Vishnu sculpture at Mamallapuram in the Avantisundari Katha. (✅ owner 11 §7, §13.4, PDF p. 87)
**Breakdown:**
- Named author: Dandin.
- Named text: Avantisundari Katha.
- Named evidence: repair of Vishnu sculpture at Mamallapuram.
- Also: Pallava bilingual inscriptions (ART-3069), Mahendravarman epithets (owner 11, PDF pp. 87, 324).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7th-8th century (owner). ⚠️ Dating of Dandin is itself debated; cite the court association, not biography.
**Links:** ART-3069, ART-3075.
**Trap:** Adding biographical detail about Dandin the owner does not supply.
**Mains hook:** The strongest Pallava citation doubles as art-history evidence.
**Back:** *"Dandin praised the Mamallapuram artisans: literature and art in one line."*

### Flashcard ART-3078 — Sanskrit and Prakrit as an on-stage social-hierarchy convention
**Front:** How did language register mark social position in classical drama?
**Definition (learn):** In Gupta-period plays, high-varna characters spoke Sanskrit while women and Shudras spoke Prakrit. The Vidusaka traditionally spoke Prakrit. This is a dramatic convention, not proof of everyday speech. (✅ owner 11 §4, §13.13 advanced §1, PDF p. 584)
**Breakdown:**
- Elite: Sanskrit.
- Women and Shudras: Prakrit.
- Vidusaka: Prakrit (ART-3029).
- Limit: Gupta-period convention; not uniform across all eras (owner 11 advanced §9).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Gupta period; PDF p. 584. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3029, ART-3065, ART-3032.
**Trap:** Generalising the convention to all Indian theatre in all periods.
**Mains hook:** Language marked social hierarchy on stage; qualify the period.
**Back:** *"Sanskrit for the high, Prakrit for women and Shudras, on stage."*

### Flashcard ART-3079 — Urdu: origin, Dakkani and early poets
**Front:** Where did Urdu develop, and what were its early names and poets?
**Definition (learn):** Urdu is an Indo-Aryan language, built on the structure of Hindi (Khari Boli) with the form and script of Persian, debated as developing through Persian-Hindi interaction. It was initially called Dakkani from its use in the Bahmani states. Amir Khusrau wrote Urdu texts in its nascent stage. (✅ owner 11 §13.5, PDF p. 686)
**Breakdown:**
- Structure: Khari Boli Hindi.
- Script: Persian.
- Early name: Dakkani (Ahmadnagar, Golconda, Bijapur, Berar).
- Early writer: Amir Khusrau.
- Owner classes it as Indo-Aryan and born in India.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 686. ⚠️ Origin in "Turkish-army barracks" is a debated claim in the owner.
**Links:** ART-3080, ART-3081, ART-3058.
**Trap:** Treating Urdu as a non-Indian import or a "Muslim language."
**Mains hook:** Urdu is an Indian language of Persian-Hindi interaction; say what the evidence shows.
**Back:** *"Urdu: Khari Boli frame, Persian script, first called Dakkani."*

### Flashcard ART-3080 — Urdu poets, genres and Sir Syed Ahmad Khan
**Front:** Name Urdu's poets, patrons and the genres the owner distinguishes.
**Definition (learn):** Mirza Ghalib (1797-1869) composed a Diwan in Urdu; other poets are Sauda, Dard and Mir Taqi Mir; Iqbal wrote Bang-e-Dara and "Sare Jahan se Achcha." Patronage came from Bahadur Shah Zafar and the Nawabs of Awadh. Sir Syed Ahmad Khan published Tahdhib-ul-Akhlaq. Genres: Qaseeda, Shahr Ashob, Ginans. (✅ owner 11 §13.5, PDF pp. 686-687)
**Breakdown:**
- Qaseeda: single presiding subject, usually praise of a king or noble.
- Shahr Ashob: narrative lament over political and social change.
- Ginans: Nizari Ismaili devotional hymns in Gujarati, Urdu, Punjabi, Sindhi.
- Sir Syed: didactic, nationalist texts; women's education; opposed purdah and polygamy.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Ghalib 1797-1869. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3079, ART-3081.
**Trap:** Calling Ginans an Urdu genre alone. They span four languages.
**Mains hook:** Use the genre and the patron to date and classify Urdu verse.
**Back:** *"Ghalib's Diwan, Sir Syed's Tahdhib, Ginans in four tongues."*

### Flashcard ART-3081 — Hindi: Apabhramsa, Bhakti boost and Prithviraj Raso
**Front:** How did Hindi develop and what are its early landmarks?
**Definition (learn):** Hindi and its dialects evolved between the 7th and 14th centuries from Apabhramsa, itself from Prakrit. Its biggest boost came from the Bhakti movement. Chand Bardai's Prithviraj Raso is probably the first Hindi book, documenting Prithviraj Chauhan. (✅ owner 11 §13.5, PDF p. 687)
**Breakdown:**
- Lineage: Prakrit, Apabhramsa, Hindi (7th-14th centuries).
- Bhakti: turned from Sanskrit to the people's language.
- Early book: Prithviraj Raso by Chand Bardai ("probably").
- Early bulk: Kabir's dohas (ART-3140).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 7th-14th centuries. ⚠️ Date of Raso is debated (owner).
**Links:** ART-3058 (Hindi as Eighth Schedule), ART-3079, ART-3140, ART-3162.
**Trap:** Calling Prithviraj Raso's date settled; the owner hedges it.
**Mains hook:** Hindi's rise is tied to Bhakti; qualify the Raso claim.
**Back:** *"Hindi from Apabhramsa; Bhakti gave the boost; Raso probably first."*

### Flashcard ART-3082 — UNESCO Memory of the World: documentary heritage and its institutions
**Front:** What is the UNESCO Memory of the World Register, and which Indian bodies hold the documentation mandate?
**Definition (learn):** UNESCO's documentary-heritage register. India's entries are on its International Register. Institutions with documentation and digitisation mandates: National Mission for Manuscripts, IGNCA, National Archives. (✅ owner 11 §2, §13.7, §13.9) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Programme: documentary heritage (not sites, not living practice).
- Count: 14 Indian entries (owner, UNESCO/UN India verified 21 July 2026).
- Custody: NMM, IGNCA, National Archives (owner).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** First international inscriptions 1997 (Wikipedia Memory of the World Programme page). ⚠️ The 14 count is owner-only; a fetched Wikipedia page did not list India's entries.
**Links:** ART-3083, ART-3084, ART-3085, ART-3086, ART-3087.
**Trap:** Treating MoW as World Heritage or ICH. Owner says they are different programmes.
**Mains hook:** Cite both inscription year and composition date for each entry.
**Back:** *"Memory of the World: documents, not sites, not living practice."*

### Flashcard ART-3083 — Rigveda 2007: inscription year is not composition date
**Front:** Why is the Rigveda's 2007 Memory of the World year not its date?
**Definition (learn):** The Rigveda is ancient. Its MoW inscription (2007) is a separate, later act of recognition. Confusing the two is the commonest error in this area. (✅ owner 11 §13.2, §13.7; advanced §2)
**Breakdown:**
- Composition: ancient, layered.
- Inscription: 2007.
- Rule: cite both separately.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2007 (owner). (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3071, ART-3082, ART-3086.
**Trap:** "The Rigveda is from 2007." No.
**Mains hook:** Use the two-date discipline in any manuscript answer.
**Back:** *"Rigveda ancient; inscription 2007; two dates, two facts."*

### Flashcard ART-3084 — MoW register diversity: colonial archives and Persian chronicles
**Front:** Which non-religious entries show that India's documentary heritage is not only scriptural?
**Definition (learn):** The Archives of the Dutch East India Company (2003) and Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah (2011), a Persian chronicle, sit alongside religious manuscripts. (✅ owner 11 §13.7, advanced §2, PDF pp. 619-621)
**Breakdown:**
- Colonial administrative: Dutch EIC archives, 2003.
- Persian chronicle: Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah, 2011.
- Folk-performance: Saang Lok Collection, 2023 (ART-3086).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2003; 2011. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3082, ART-3085, ART-3086.
**Trap:** "All Indian MoW entries are ancient religious texts."
**Mains hook:** Name at least two registers to show range.
**Back:** *"Dutch archives 2003, Timurid chronicle 2011: not only scripture."*

### Flashcard ART-3085 — MoW 2024-2025 entries
**Front:** Which Indian MoW entries were inscribed in 2024 and 2025?
**Definition (learn):** 2024: Ramcharitmanas, Panchatantra, Sahrdayaloka-Locana. 2025: manuscripts of the Bhagavadgita and the Natyashastra (UNESCO/UN India, per owner). (✅ owner 11 §2, §13.7, PDF pp. 619-621) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- 2024: Ramcharitmanas (Tulsidas, Awadhi), Panchatantra (Sanskrit fables), Sahrdayaloka-Locana (aesthetics).
- 2025: Bhagavadgita, Natyashastra manuscripts.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2024; 2025. ⚠️ Owner-only; the 2025 additions are not confirmed by a fetched source.
**Links:** ART-3144 (Ramcharitmanas in file 13), ART-3082, ART-3083.
**Trap:** Reading 2025 as composition date of the Gita.
**Mains hook:** Recognition in 2025 does not date the text.
**Back:** *"2024: Ramcharitmanas, Panchatantra, Sahrdayaloka; 2025: Gita, Natyashastra."*

### Flashcard ART-3086 — MoW 1997-2023 entries
**Front:** Name the earlier named MoW entries and their years.
**Definition (learn):** I.A.S. Tamil Medical Manuscript Collection (1997); Shaiva Manuscript in Pondicherry (2005); Dutch East India Company archives (2003); Tarikh-e-Khandan-e-Timuriyah and Laghukalachakratantrarajatika Vimalaprabha (2011); Shantinatha Charitra (2013); Gilgit Manuscript and Maitreyavyakarana (2017); Abhinavagupta Manuscripts and Saang Lok Collection (2023). (✅ owner 11 §2, §13.7)
**Breakdown:**
- 1997 Tamil medical; 2003 Dutch archives; 2005 Shaiva manuscript.
- 2011 Persian chronicle; Buddhist/Kalachakra commentary.
- 2013 Jain Shantinatha Charitra.
- 2017 Gilgit (Buddhist), Maitreyavyakarana (Buddhist).
- 2023 Abhinavagupta; Saang Lok (folk performance).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Years as listed. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3084, ART-3085, ART-3082.
**Trap:** Reading 1997 as the date of the oldest item in the list. It is the register-year of the earliest entry, not an age.
**Mains hook:** Use the range of registers (Buddhist, Jain, folk, medical).
**Back:** *"1997 medical, 2005 Shaiva, 2013 Jain, 2017 Buddhist, 2023 folk and aesthetics."*

### Flashcard ART-3087 — Programme firewall: MoW, WHS, ICH and GI
**Front:** Distinguish Memory of the World, World Heritage, Intangible Cultural Heritage and Geographical Indication.
**Definition (learn):** MoW is documentary heritage. World Heritage is tangible sites. ICH is living practice. GI is domestic intellectual property for goods. (✅ owner 11 §9, §13.7; owner 12 §13.4)
**Breakdown:**
- MoW: documents.
- WHS: sites.
- ICH: living practice.
- GI: origin-linked goods, domestic IP law.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3082, ART-3019, ART-3020, ART-3092.
**Trap:** Treating MoW inscription as WHS or ICH status.
**Mains hook:** State which programme you mean before giving a date.
**Back:** *"Documents, sites, living practice, goods: four lists, four rules."*

### Flashcard ART-3088 — 2026 Prelims Q7: place-value notation (provisional key)
**Front:** What does the 2026 Prelims GS-I Q7 test?
**Definition (learn):** Place-value notation in Indian epigraphic and Southeast Asian inscriptions. The 2026 Set-A key is provisional and no answer letter is recorded. (✅ owner 11 2026 PYQ block) ⚠️
**Breakdown:**
- Subject: place-value notation in inscriptions.
- Status: provisional key; answer not inferred.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2026 Q7. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3061, ART-3082.
**Trap:** Stating a letter from a provisional key.
**Mains hook:** Epigraphic numerals connect language and inscription.
**Back:** *"2026 Q7: numerals in inscriptions; key still provisional."*

### Flashcard ART-3089 — Status comparison: classical, Scheduled, official and national
**Front:** Compare the four language statuses that candidates confuse.
**Definition (learn):** Classical language: honorific recognition (11 after October 2024). Eighth Schedule: 22 Scheduled Languages. Official language of the Union: Hindi, Article 343. "National language": no constitutional declaration exists. (✅ owner 11 §13.6, §13.9) ⚠️ classical count owner-only.
**Breakdown:**
- Classical: ART-3060.
- Eighth Schedule: ART-3058.
- Official: ART-3059.
- National: none.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 22; 11; Article 343. (✅ owner 11)
**Links:** ART-3057, ART-3058, ART-3059, ART-3060.
**Trap:** Mixing all four into one "official language" answer.
**Mains hook:** Name the status, then the consequence.
**Back:** *"Classical honours, Eighth Schedule lists, Article 343 sets official, no national."*

### GROUP — Crafts, Textiles, Folk and Tribal Traditions

### Flashcard ART-3090 — Craft ecology: material, skill, community, market, legal recognition (concept card)
**Front:** What is the craft-ecology chain, and where does a GI tag intervene?
**Definition (learn):** Craft survives through a chain: material, skill or technique, producing community, market access, legal recognition (GI), and safeguarding. A GI tag intervenes only at the legal-recognition link. (✅ owner 12 §13.6, advanced §2, §5)
**Breakdown:**
- Material and skill: craft form.
- Community: producing and transmitting group.
- Market: patron, merchant, export, tourism.
- Legal link: GI (ART-3092 to ART-3098).
- Safeguarding: institutions (owner 12 cross-link to file 14).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** Owner-only analytical chain. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3092, ART-3119, ART-3122, ART-3123.
**Trap:** Treating a GI as a full safeguarding programme.
**Mains hook:** Argue along the chain and name the link that fails.
**Back:** *"Material, skill, people, market, law: GI fixes only the law."*

### Flashcard ART-3091 — Handicraft: definition and material classes
**Front:** How does the owner define a handicraft and classify craft materials?
**Definition (learn):** A handicraft is an amalgamation of all things crafted by hand, serving as livelihood and cultural heritage, especially for tribal and rural communities. Material classes: glassware, cloth handicrafts, ivory carving, silver crafts, clay and pottery, bronze crafts, other metal crafts, leather products, wooden work, toys, stoneware, floor designs. (✅ owner 12 §2, §3, PDF pp. 378)
**Breakdown:**
- Definition: hand-made, livelihood plus heritage.
- Classes: twelve material-wise groups as listed.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 378. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3090, ART-3107, ART-3108.
**Trap:** Using the material list as a list of states.
**Mains hook:** The book's material classes are a ready answer spine.
**Back:** *"Glass, cloth, ivory, silver, clay, bronze, metal, leather, wood, toys, stone, floors."*

### Flashcard ART-3092 — GI: a sign of regional origin, not a quality mark
**Front:** What does a Geographical Indication protect?
**Definition (learn):** A sign that an item has originated from a particular region only. It ensures that only registered authorised users may use the product name. (✅ owner 12 §2, PDF p. 406)
**Breakdown:**
- Protects: product name for authorised users.
- Does not certify quality or grant heritage status.
- Domestic IP under the GI Act.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 406. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3093, ART-3094, ART-3095, ART-3087.
**Trap:** "A GI is a quality certificate or a heritage designation." Owner rejects both.
**Mains hook:** GI is narrow origin-linked IP; say what it does not secure.
**Back:** *"GI protects a name's origin, not a craft's welfare."*

### Flashcard ART-3093 — GI Act 1999: in force 2003, goods only
**Front:** Which Act governs GI in India, when did it come into force, and what does it cover?
**Definition (learn):** The Geographical Indications of Goods (Registration and Protection) Act, 1999, in force since 2003, covers goods only, not services. (✅ Wikipedia: Geographical indications in India; ✅ owner 12 §3, PDF p. 407)
**Breakdown:**
- Act: 1999.
- In force: 15 September 2003 (Wikipedia).
- Scope: goods, not services.
- Not a UNESCO category (owner 12 §13.4).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 1999; 15 September 2003. (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3092, ART-3094, ART-3087.
**Trap:** Dating the Act to its enactment year when the question asks effective year. Or saying it covers services.
**Mains hook:** Cite the Act, scope and in-force date together.
**Back:** *"1999 Act, 2003 in force, goods only."*

### Flashcard ART-3094 — GI Registry, validity and fee
**Front:** Where is the GI Registry located, under which ministry, and what are the validity and fee?
**Definition (learn):** The GI Registry (Chennai) issues GI certification under DPIIT, Ministry of Commerce and Industry. Registration is valid for 10 years, renewable every 10 years. Certification covers 34 classes. Application fee Rs. 5,000. (✅ owner 12 §2, §13.4, PDF p. 407)
**Breakdown:**
- Location: Chennai.
- Ministry: DPIIT, Commerce and Industry.
- Validity: 10 years, renewable.
- Classes: 34.
- Fee: Rs. 5,000.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** As listed. ⚠️ Fee and class count are owner-only; the Wikipedia fetch did not confirm them. Verify against the Registry.
**Links:** ART-3093, ART-3097.
**Trap:** Saying GI is permanent. It must be renewed.
**Mains hook:** Renewal is the point most often missed.
**Back:** *"Chennai registry, 10-year term, renew to keep."*

### Flashcard ART-3095 — WIPO's three GI protection methods
**Front:** What are the three methods of GI protection that WIPO identifies, and which does India use?
**Definition (learn):** (1) A law designed exclusively for GIs (sui generis); (2) certification or collective marks; (3) general business-practice and consumer-protection law. India uses the sui generis Act. (✅ owner 12 §2, §13.4, PDF p. 408)
**Breakdown:**
- Sui generis: a law specific to GIs (India's route, ART-3093).
- Certification or collective marks.
- Business-practice and consumer law.
- International frameworks: Paris Convention; WTO TRIPS (owner 12 §5).
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** PDF p. 408. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3093, ART-3116 (certification versus GI).
**Trap:** Calling Oeko-Tex a GI method. It is a voluntary testing standard (ART-3116).
**Mains hook:** A comparative IP frame for 20-mark answers.
**Back:** *"Sui generis, certification marks, consumer law: India chose sui generis."*

### Flashcard ART-3096 — Darjeeling Tea: India's first GI-tagged product
**Front:** Which product was India's first GI-tagged product, and when?
**Definition (learn):** Darjeeling Tea, registered 2004-05. (✅ Wikipedia: Geographical indications in India; ✅ owner 12 §4, PDF p. 407)
**Breakdown:**
- Product: Darjeeling Tea.
- Year: 2004-05.
- Owner's Mains use: opening fact for any GI answer.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 2004-05. (✅ Wikipedia; ✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3093, ART-3097, ART-3098.
**Trap:** Naming Kangra Tea or Nilambur Teak as first.
**Mains hook:** Open with Darjeeling and the year.
**Back:** *"Darjeeling Tea first, 2004-05."*

### Flashcard ART-3097 — GI totals and leading state (dated)
**Front:** How many goods held GI status, and which state led, and as of when?
**Definition (learn):** The book reports 658 registered goods as of July 2025. Uttar Pradesh held the most GI tags (77) as of April 2025, surpassing Tamil Nadu's previous lead. (✅ owner 12 §4, §8, §13.9)
**Breakdown:**
- Total: 658 (July 2025).
- Leader: Uttar Pradesh, 77 (April 2025).
- Previous leader: Tamil Nadu.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** 658 (July 2025); 77 (April 2025). (✅ owner 12) ⚠️ Not verified by a fetched source; owner-dated only.
**Links:** ART-3094, ART-3096.
**Trap:** Presenting 658 or 77 as today's figure, or saying Tamil Nadu leads now.
**Mains hook:** Close with a dated extract or parliamentary reply, never a round number.
**Back:** *"658 in July 2025; UP 77 in April 2025, past Tamil Nadu."*

### Flashcard ART-3098 — Kangra Tea (EU), Nilambur Teak and domestic GI abroad
**Front:** Which Indian GIs have cross-border recognition, and what is the rule?
**Definition (learn):** Kangra Tea (Himachal Pradesh) secured protected GI status in the European Union. Kerala's Nilambur Teak holds a GI. A domestic GI does not travel automatically; each foreign protection needs separate registration. (✅ owner 12 §4, §13.4, PDF pp. 407-408)
**Breakdown:**
- Kangra Tea: EU protection.
- Nilambur Teak: domestic GI, Kerala.
- Rule: separate registration abroad.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner. ⚠️ EU year not given in owner.
**Links:** ART-3096, ART-3093.
**Trap:** "Indian GI is automatically protected abroad."
**Mains hook:** Kangra Tea answers the question of cross-border validity.
**Back:** *"Kangra in the EU by separate filing; domestic GI stops at the border."*

### Flashcard ART-3099 — Patola: double ikat from Patan
**Front:** What is Patola, and where is it made?
**Definition (learn):** A double-ikat silk textile from Patan, Gujarat, resist-dyed in both warp and weft. (✅ owner 12 §4, §13.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Region: Patan, Gujarat.
- Technique: double ikat.
- Not Pochampally, not Kanchipuram.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None beyond owner. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3100, ART-3101.
**Trap:** Calling Patola Pochampally. Pochampally is single or double ikat in Telangana.
**Mains hook:** Name region, process and product together.
**Back:** *"Patola: Patan, double ikat, both threads."*

### Flashcard ART-3100 — Pochampally ikat
**Front:** What is Pochampally ikat and where is it?
**Definition (learn):** Resist-dyed yarn from Telangana; may be single or double ikat. (✅ owner 12 §4, §13.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Region: Telangana.
- Technique: ikat (single or double).
- Discriminator: different centre and pattern logic from Patola.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3099, ART-3101.
**Trap:** Saying Pochampally is always double ikat.
**Mains hook:** Ikat is a family; the centre names the type.
**Back:** *"Pochampally: Telangana ikat, single or double."*

### Flashcard ART-3101 — Kanchipuram silk: border joining
**Front:** What is the marker of Kanchipuram silk?
**Definition (learn):** Silk from Tamil Nadu in which contrasting borders are woven and joined to the body. (✅ owner 12 §4, §13.5)
**Breakdown:**
- Region: Tamil Nadu.
- Technique: silk weaving, border joining.
- Marker: the joining technique.
**Key facts / Dates / Data:** None. (✅ owner 12)
**Links:** ART-3099, ART-3102.
**Trap:** Calling it ikat. Its marker is the border join.
**Mains hook:** Name the joining method as evidence.
**Back:** *"Kanchipuram: silk, border joined to body."*

### Flashcard ART-3102 — Banarasi brocade: supplementary-weft zari
**Front:** What is Banarasi brocade?
